Yes, even when she says it's just regular talk, I know better. They're telling me to be careful, plain as day. I just wish they wouldn't beat around the bush so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the messages you're receiving are part of a bigger plan or that someone is trying to influence your thoughts or actions in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, a bigger plan... I suppose you could call it that, yes. Like I said, the men at the domino table and the radio announcer, they're giving me these little messages. It's meant for me, to warn me to be careful. My wife tells me it's just talk, but I know it's not. I know it. \n\nSpeaking of plans, though, my wife's been planning to redo the back porch. We've had that same screened-in porch since we bought the place. I put the screens up myself, you know. Bought the mesh down at the old hardware store on Main Street. Old man Henderson ran it back then. He had this big jar of peppermints on the counter, the kind wrapped in clear plastic. You don't see those much anymore. Now everything is in a big box store, no personal touch. Henderson knew exactly what size nails you needed just by looking at the wood you brought in. \n\nBut, uh, yes, the messages. They are definitely trying to tell me something, trying to get me to be careful about... well, whatever it is they're warning me about. I just know it's real, no matter what my wife says.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the messages you're getting are all connected, like they're part of a single plan or scheme that someone has put together specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Well, connected... I suppose they must be. I mean, Charlie at the domino table and the fella on the radio, they're both doing it, right? Giving me those little messages meant just for me. Warning me to be careful. My wife, she just shakes her head and says it's regular talk, but I know what I hear. I know they're warning me. \n\nYou know, speaking of being careful, I used to tell my boys that all the time when they were learning to drive. We had this old Ford pickup, a '54, painted the color of a bruised apple. The clutch on that thing was stiffer than a frozen boot. You had to really stomp on it, or it would grind the gears something awful. My oldest, Tommy, he ground 'em so bad once I thought the whole transmission was gonna fall right out onto the dirt road. We used to drive down to the creek to fish for catfish. Used stink bait... boy, did that stuff smell. Get it on your hands and you wouldn't get it off for a week. \n\nBut yes, doctor, the messages. They're connected, sure as I'm sitting here. They're all trying to tell me the same thing, to watch my step. Even if my wife doesn't hear it, I know they're warning me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the messages you're hearing are being put into your mind by someone or something else, like through the radio or when you're playing dominoes?\n\nPatient: Put into my mind? No, no, they don't have to get inside my head to do it. Like I was telling you, doctor, I came in here because the men at the domino table and sometimes the radio announcer give these little messages meant for me. It's right there out in the open. And even when my wife says it is just talk, I know they are warning me to be careful. I just hear it with my own two ears, plain as day. \n\nMy hearing's always been sharp, you know. Never needed one of those hearing aids. Now my brother Frank, he went deaf as a post by the time he was sixty. Worked in a metal stamping plant up north. Big heavy presses coming down all day long—bam, bam, bam. Shook the whole concrete floor. They didn't give 'em earplugs back then, you just stuffed a bit of cotton in there if your head started ringing too bad. Frank ended up having to read lips mostly. He was a crackerjack mechanic, though. Could rebuild a carburetor blindfolded. Used to lay all the little springs and screws out on a clean rag right on the kitchen table. His wife, Mary, she'd get so mad about the smell of gasoline in the house... she'd chase him out to the garage with a broom. They had a nice garage, detached, with a little wood stove in the corner. Kept it real warm in the winter.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the messages you're hearing are being placed into your thoughts by someone or something else, or do you experience them as coming from outside yourself, like through the radio or during conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, they're not putting thoughts in my head. It's coming from outside, right out in the open. The men at the domino table, the fella on the radio... they just give these little messages meant for me. I hear them talking, warning me to be careful. My wife, she keeps telling me it's just regular talk, but I know what I'm hearing. \n\nSpeaking of hearing things, we used to have this old Zenith radio in the living room. Big wooden cabinet, took up half the wall. You had to wait for the tubes to warm up before you could hear anything. We'd sit around it on Sunday evenings and listen to the programs. Jack Benny, The Lone Ranger... those were the days. My mother would be knitting in her chair, always making these scratchy wool sweaters. I hated wearing them, they made my neck itch terribly. She'd use this dark green yarn she got on sale down at the five and dime. The lady who worked there, Mrs. Gable, she had a glass eye. Always stared right through you. She gave me a piece of hard candy once, but it tasted like mothballs. \n\nBut, uh, no, nobody's putting thoughts in my head. I just hear the messages they're sending me to be careful, plain as day.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the messages you're hearing are part of a special mission or that you have been chosen for a specific purpose?\n\nPatient: Well, a special mission... I don't know if I'd call it a mission, exactly, but sometimes I get so sure that I'm the only one who can bring the whole neighborhood together again. It's like I have this special gift, you know? A gift for making people listen and follow my plans. I just know I can fix things around here if they'd just pay attention. \n\nWe used to have block parties every summer when I was younger. The whole street would come out. Mrs. Higgins would make her famous potato salad. It had too much mustard for my taste, but everyone else seemed to love it. She lived in that blue house on the corner, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree was struck by lightning back in '78. Split it right down the middle, sounded like a cannon going off. Woke the whole street up. The fire department had to come out because a branch fell on old Mr. Peterson's Buick. Ruined the roof completely. He was so mad he tried to sue the city, but you can't sue God for lightning, can you? I just know it's real, no matter what my wife says.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the messages you're getting are all connected, like they're part of a single plan or scheme that someone has put together specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Well, connected... I suppose they must be. I mean, Charlie at the domino table and the fella on the radio, they're both doing it, right? Giving me those little messages meant just for me. Warning me to be careful. My wife, she just shakes her head and says it's regular talk, but I know what I hear. I know they're warning me. \n\nYou know, speaking of being careful, I used to tell my boys that all the time when they were learning to drive. We had this old Ford pickup, a '54, painted the color of a bruised apple. The clutch on that thing was stiffer than a frozen boot. You had to really stomp on it, or it would grind the gears something awful. My oldest, Tommy, he ground 'em so bad once I thought the whole transmission was gonna fall right out onto the dirt road. We used to drive down to the creek to fish for catfish. Used stink bait... boy, did that stuff smell. Get it on your hands and you wouldn't get it off for a week. \n\nBut yes, doctor, the messages. They're connected, sure as I'm sitting here. They're all trying to tell me the same thing, to watch my step. Even if my wife doesn't hear it, I know they're warning me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the messages you're hearing are being put into your mind by someone or something else, like through the radio or when you're playing dominoes?\n\nPatient: Put into my mind? No, no, they don't have to get inside my head to do it. Like I was telling you, doctor, I came in here because the men at the domino table and sometimes the radio announcer give these little messages meant for me. It's right there out in the open. And even when my wife says it is just talk, I know they are warning me to be careful. I just hear it with my own two ears, plain as day. \n\nMy hearing's always been sharp, you know. Never needed one of those hearing aids. Now my brother Frank, he went deaf as a post by the time he was sixty. Worked in a metal stamping plant up north. Big heavy presses coming down all day long—bam, bam, bam. Shook the whole concrete floor. They didn't give 'em earplugs back then, you just stuffed a bit of cotton in there if your head started ringing too bad. Frank ended up having to read lips mostly. He was a crackerjack mechanic, though. Could rebuild a carburetor blindfolded. Used to lay all the little springs and screws out on a clean rag right on the kitchen table. His wife, Mary, she'd get so mad about the smell of gasoline in the house... she'd chase him out to the garage with a broom. They had a nice garage, detached, with a little wood stove in the corner. Kept it real warm in the winter.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the messages you're hearing are being placed into your thoughts by someone or something else, or do you experience them as coming from outside yourself, like through the radio or during conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, they're not putting thoughts in my head. It's coming from outside, right out in the open. The men at the domino table, the fella on the radio... they just give these little messages meant for me. I hear them talking, warning me to be careful. My wife, she keeps telling me it's just regular talk, but I know what I'm hearing. \n\nSpeaking of hearing things, we used to have this old Zenith radio in the living room. Big wooden cabinet, took up half the wall. You had to wait for the tubes to warm up before you could hear anything. We'd sit around it on Sunday evenings and listen to the programs. Jack Benny, The Lone Ranger... those were the days. My mother would be knitting in her chair, always making these scratchy wool sweaters. I hated wearing them, they made my neck itch terribly. She'd use this dark green yarn she got on sale down at the five and dime. The lady who worked there, Mrs. Gable, she had a glass eye. Always stared right through you. She gave me a piece of hard candy once, but it tasted like mothballs. \n\nBut, uh, no, nobody's putting thoughts in my head. I just hear the messages they're sending me to be careful, plain as day.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the messages you're hearing are part of a special mission or that you have been chosen for a specific purpose?\n\nPatient: Well, a special mission... I don't know if I'd call it a mission, exactly, but sometimes I get so sure that I'm the only one who can bring the whole neighborhood together again. It's like I have this special gift, you know? A gift for making people listen and follow my plans. I just know I can fix things around here if they'd just pay attention. \n\nWe used to have block parties every summer when I was younger. The whole street would come out. Mrs. Higgins would make her famous potato salad. It had too much mustard for my taste, but everyone else seemed to love it. She lived in that blue house on the corner, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree was struck by lightning back in '78. Split it right down the middle, sounded like a cannon going off. Woke the whole street up. The fire department had to come out because a branch fell on old Mr. Peterson's Buick. Ruined the roof completely. He was so mad he tried to sue the city, but you can't sue God for lightning, can you? That's what the judge told him anyway.\n\nBut yeah, I just feel like I've got this... this ability to get people organized, to get them to follow my lead. Like a special gift.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that you have any special powers or abilities that other people don't have?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it's not like I can fly or read minds or anything out of a comic book. But I do get sure, I mean really sure, that I am the one who can bring the whole neighborhood together again. It's like I have a special gift for making people listen and follow my plans. \n\nI was actually trying to explain this to the mailman the other day. He's a new fella, young kid, maybe twenty-two. Drives one of those new electric mail trucks. They're so quiet, you don't even hear them coming down the street. Not like the old ones that used to rattle and sputter. My dog, Buster—God rest his soul—used to hear the mail truck from three blocks away and start howling. Buster was a good dog, part beagle, part something else. We found him shivering under a parked car in the grocery store parking lot. The old A&P that used to be down on Main Street. They tore it down in the nineties to build a pharmacy, which is funny because now there's a pharmacy on every corner. You can't throw a rock without hitting a place selling aspirin and greeting cards. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just have this gift, you know? A way of getting people to see my plans and follow along.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have any kind of special connection with a higher power or that you've been chosen by God or any divine figure for a specific purpose?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I'm not a prophet or anything. I mean, I believe in the good Lord, sure, but He hasn't come down to give me any special orders or tap me on the shoulder. \n\nWe used to go to St. Jude's every Sunday, though. Beautiful old church. They had these big stained glass windows all along the east wall. One of them got broke back in... oh, must have been '89. A bunch of kids playing baseball in the empty lot next door hit a foul ball right through the face of Saint Peter. The priest, Father Thomas, he was furious. He had this little vein in his forehead that would bulge out when he got mad. He drove a Ford Pinto, you know. Pale yellow. Worst car ever made, those Pintos. My brother-in-law Charlie bought one brand new and the heater never worked. Charlie was a pipefitter down at the plant, always had cold hands anyway. He used to carry a big metal thermos of black coffee just to keep his fingers warm on the job. Black coffee, no sugar. Said sugar was for hummingbirds. He had a glass hummingbird feeder on his back porch, used to boil the sugar water himself on the stove... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, God's not giving me any special divine purpose or talking to me from on high. Just my own gift for getting the neighborhood together, that's all.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that you are better or more important than other people, or that you have a role that no one else can fulfill?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about being more important, like I'm a king or a mayor or something. But I do get this feeling, this absolute certainty, that I'm the only one who can bring the whole neighborhood together again. Like I've got this special gift for making folks listen and follow my plans. \n\nIt's sort of like how my Uncle Frank used to get everyone to listen to him. He was a loud man, Frank. Worked on the railroad for forty years, laying track out west. He lost half of his left pinky finger in a coupling accident back in '52. Never stopped him from playing the accordion, though. He'd bring that heavy old thing out at Christmas and play polkas until my aunt would threaten to hide it in the attic. She kept all her old sewing patterns up in that attic. Hundreds of them, mostly from the sixties. She made me a suit once, for my cousin's wedding. It was a terrible shade of brown, like mud, but I wore it anyway because she worked so hard on it. The wedding was down at the VFW hall, the one with the leaky roof over the dance floor... \n\nBut yeah, I just know I'm the one who can get everyone on the same page. It's my special gift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that you have a unique role or importance that others don't, like you're meant to lead or guide people in a way no one else can?\n\nPatient: Well, like I keep telling you, Doc, I ain't no president or nothing. But I just got this... this absolute knowing that I'm the one meant to bring the whole neighborhood together. People just naturally listen to me and want to follow the plans I make. \n\nYou gotta have good plans, you know. You can't build a house without a blueprint. My daddy was a carpenter, built half the houses on the south side of town with his own two hands. He used one of those wooden folding rulers, kept it right in his overalls pocket. Those overalls always smelled like pine sawdust and chewing tobacco. Red Man, that was his brand. He'd spit it right into an old Folgers coffee can he kept by the porch steps. Mother hated that can. She used to grow petunias all around the porch, bright pink ones. Said the soil was good there because of the rain runoff from the gutters. We had to clean those gutters out every autumn, wet leaves piled up thick from the big oak tree out front. That tree got struck by lightning back in the summer of '65, split right down the middle like a piece of firewood... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. I just know I'm the one to lead the neighborhood. It's my gift.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know, it depends on the week. Some weeks I wake up feeling so bright and important, like I've got the sun right in my chest. I laugh loud, I talk fast, and I'm just ready to get things moving. But if folks don't listen, if they slow me down or start questioning my plans, I get real sharp with them. Real snappy. 

It reminds me of how my old dog Buster used to get. Buster was a beagle mix, had one blue eye and one brown eye. Smartest dog you ever saw, but he had no patience for the neighbor's cat. That cat was a big orange tabby, looked like a pumpkin with legs. It used to sit right on the fence post and just stare at Buster. Used to drive him crazy. Buster would bark until his voice went hoarse. We kept him on a long chain in the yard, attached to this old metal stake my daddy drove into the ground. Daddy used a sledgehammer for that. Heavy thing, probably weighed twenty pounds. He bought it at the hardware store downtown, the one Mr. Henderson used to run before he retired and moved to Florida. Florida's too hot, if you ask me. All that humidity, makes your joints ache. My cousin Martha went to Florida once for a vacation, brought back a whole bag of those little seashell soaps. Looked pretty, but they didn't lather worth a darn... 

But, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, when I'm feeling bright and fast, I just get irritable if people don't keep up with me or question what I'm doing.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22.0, F22.8, F22.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, my wife is the one who wanted me to come talk to you. It’s mostly about the men down at the domino table. I’ve been playing with those boys for... oh, must be twenty years now, ever since I stopped working. Charlie, he always tries to hide the double six, but I catch him. His hands are all curled up with arthritis, poor fella, so he fumbles the tiles a lot. \n\nBut lately... well, I came because they've been slipping in these little messages when they play, and they're meant just for me. And it's not just them, it's the radio announcer too. I listen to the AM station every morning. You know, they don't make radios like they used to. My daddy had one of those big wooden ones, took up half the parlor, had the glowing tubes in the back... \n\nBut anyway, the fella on the radio, he does it too. He gives me messages. My wife, she gets all huffed up and says it is just regular talk, but I know better. I know they are warning me to be careful. I just... I don't know why they can't just come out and say it plain instead of talking in codes.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the messages you receive from the men at the domino table and the radio announcer? Do you believe they are specifically targeting you or trying to harm you in some way?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think they want to hurt me. It's more like... like a warning. Charlie will play a tile, maybe a four-blank, and the way he taps it on the table—tap, tap, tap—it means I need to watch my back. And the radio fella, he'll be reading the farm report or the weather, and he'll pause just a second too long before he says 'storm.' It's meant for me. It's a sign to be careful. \n\nMy wife, bless her heart, she just sighs and tells me to turn the dial. She never did like AM radio anyway. Always preferred her gospel records. The water was so clear back then, not like now with all the algae and the motorboats making a racket. Yes, even when she says it's just regular talk, I know better. They're telling me to be careful, plain as day. I just wish they wouldn't beat around the bush so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the messages you're receiving are part of a bigger plan or that someone is trying to influence your thoughts or actions in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, a bigger plan... I suppose you could call it that, yes. Like I said, the men at the domino table and the radio announcer, they're giving me these little messages. It's meant for me, to warn me to be careful. My wife tells me it's just talk, but I know it's not. I know it. \n\nSpeaking of plans, though, my wife's been planning to redo the back porch. We've had that same screened-in porch since we bought the place. I put the screens up myself, you know. Bought the mesh down at the old hardware store on Main Street. Now everything is in a big box store, no personal touch. Henderson knew exactly what size nails you needed just by looking at the wood you brought in. \n\nBut, uh, yes, the messages. They are definitely trying to tell me something, trying to get me to be careful about... well, whatever it is they're warning me about. I just know it's real, no matter what my wife says.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the messages you're getting are all connected, like they're part of a single plan or scheme that someone has put together specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Well, connected... I suppose they must be. I mean, Charlie at the domino table and the fella on the radio, they're both doing it, right? Giving me those little messages meant just for me. Warning me to be careful. My wife, she just shakes her head and says it's regular talk, but I know what I hear. I know they're warning me. \n\nYou know, speaking of being careful, I used to tell my boys that all the time when they were learning to drive. We had this old Ford pickup, a '54, painted the color of a bruised apple. The clutch on that thing was stiffer than a frozen boot. You had to really stomp on it, or it would grind the gears something awful. My oldest, Tommy, he ground 'em so bad once I thought the whole transmission was gonna fall right out onto the dirt road. We used to drive down to the creek to fish for catfish. Used stink bait... boy, did that stuff smell. Get it on your hands and you wouldn't get it off for a week. \n\nBut yes, doctor, the messages. They're connected, sure as I'm sitting here. They're all trying to tell me the same thing, to watch my step. Even if my wife doesn't hear it, I know they're warning me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the messages you're hearing are being put into your mind by someone or something else, like through the radio or when you're playing dominoes?\n\nPatient: Put into my mind? No, no, they don't have to get inside my head to do it. Like I was telling you, doctor, I came in here because the men at the domino table and sometimes the radio announcer give these little messages meant for me. It's right there out in the open. And even when my wife says it is just talk, I know they are warning me to be careful. I just hear it with my own two ears, plain as day. \n\nMy hearing's always been sharp, you know. Never needed one of those hearing aids. Now my brother Frank, he went deaf as a post by the time he was sixty. Worked in a metal stamping plant up north. Big heavy presses coming down all day long—bam, bam, bam. Shook the whole concrete floor. They didn't give 'em earplugs back then, you just stuffed a bit of cotton in there if your head started ringing too bad. Frank ended up having to read lips mostly. He was a crackerjack mechanic, though. Could rebuild a carburetor blindfolded. Used to lay all the little springs and screws out on a clean rag right on the kitchen table. His wife, Mary, she'd get so mad about the smell of gasoline in the house... she'd chase him out to the garage with a broom. They had a nice garage, detached, with a little wood stove in the corner. Kept it real warm in the winter.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the messages you're hearing are being placed into your thoughts by someone or something else, or do you experience them as coming from outside yourself, like through the radio or during conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, they're not putting thoughts in my head. It's coming from outside, right out in the open. The men at the domino table, the fella on the radio... they just give these little messages meant for me. I hear them talking, warning me to be careful. My wife, she keeps telling me it's just regular talk, but I know what I'm hearing. \n\nSpeaking of hearing things, we used to have this old Zenith radio in the living room. Big wooden cabinet, took up half the wall. You had to wait for the tubes to warm up before you could hear anything. We'd sit around it on Sunday evenings and listen to the programs. Jack Benny, The Lone Ranger... those were the days. My mother would be knitting in her chair, always making these scratchy wool sweaters. I hated wearing them, they made my neck itch terribly. She'd use this dark green yarn she got on sale down at the five and dime. The lady who worked there, Mrs. Gable, she had a glass eye. Always stared right through you. She gave me a piece of hard candy once, but it tasted like mothballs. \n\nBut, uh, no, nobody's putting thoughts in my head. I just hear the messages they're sending me to be careful, plain as day.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the messages you're hearing are part of a special mission or that you have been chosen for a specific purpose?\n\nPatient: Well, a special mission... I don't know if I'd call it a mission, exactly, but sometimes I get so sure that I'm the only one who can bring the whole neighborhood together again. It's like I have this special gift, you know? A gift for making people listen and follow my plans. I just know I can fix things around here if they'd just pay attention. \n\nWe used to have block parties every summer when I was younger. The whole street would come out. Mrs. Higgins would make her famous potato salad. It had too much mustard for my taste, but everyone else seemed to love it. She lived in that blue house on the corner, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree was struck by lightning back in '78. Split it right down the middle, sounded like a cannon going off. Woke the whole street up. The fire department had to come out because a branch fell on old Mr. Peterson's Buick. Ruined the roof completely. He was so mad he tried to sue the city, but you can't sue God for lightning, can you? That's what the judge told him anyway.\n\nBut yeah, I just feel like I've got this... this ability to get people organized, to get them to follow my lead. Like a special gift.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that you have any special powers or abilities that other people don't have?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it's not like I can fly or read minds or anything out of a comic book. But I do get sure, I mean really sure, that I am the one who can bring the whole neighborhood together again. It's like I have a special gift for making people listen and follow my plans. \n\nI was actually trying to explain this to the mailman the other day. He's a new fella, young kid, maybe twenty-two. Drives one of those new electric mail trucks. They're so quiet, you don't even hear them coming down the street. Not like the old ones that used to rattle and sputter. My dog, Buster—God rest his soul—used to hear the mail truck from three blocks away and start howling. Buster was a good dog, part beagle, part something else. We found him shivering under a parked car in the grocery store parking lot. The old A&P that used to be down on Main Street. They tore it down in the nineties to build a pharmacy, which is funny because now there's a pharmacy on every corner. You can't throw a rock without hitting a place selling aspirin and greeting cards. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just have this gift, you know? A way of getting people to see my plans and follow along.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have any kind of special connection with a higher power or that you've been chosen by God or any divine figure for a specific purpose?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I'm not a prophet or anything. I mean, I believe in the good Lord, sure, but He hasn't come down to give me any special orders or tap me on the shoulder. \n\nWe used to go to St. Jude's every Sunday, though. Beautiful old church. They had these big stained glass windows all along the east wall. One of them got broke back in... oh, must have been '89. A bunch of kids playing baseball in the empty lot next door hit a foul ball right through the face of Saint Peter. The priest, Father Thomas, he was furious. He had this little vein in his forehead that would bulge out when he got mad. He drove a Ford Pinto, you know. Pale yellow. Worst car ever made, those Pintos. My brother-in-law Charlie bought one brand new and the heater never worked. Charlie was a pipefitter down at the plant, always had cold hands anyway. He used to carry a big metal thermos of black coffee just to keep his fingers warm on the job. Black coffee, no sugar. Said sugar was for hummingbirds. He had a glass hummingbird feeder on his back porch, used to boil the sugar water himself on the stove... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, God's not giving me any special divine purpose or talking to me from on high. Just my own gift for getting the neighborhood together, that's all.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that you are better or more important than other people, or that you have a role that no one else can fulfill?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about being more important, like I'm a king or a mayor or something. But I do get this feeling, this absolute certainty, that I'm the only one who can bring the whole neighborhood together again. Like I've got this special gift for making folks listen and follow my plans. \n\nIt's sort of like how my Uncle Frank used to get everyone to listen to him. He was a loud man, Frank. Worked on the railroad for forty years, laying track out west. He lost half of his left pinky finger in a coupling accident back in '52. Never stopped him from playing the accordion, though. He'd bring that heavy old thing out at Christmas and play polkas until my aunt would threaten to hide it in the attic. She kept all her old sewing patterns up in that attic. Hundreds of them, mostly from the sixties. She made me a suit once, for my cousin's wedding. It was a terrible shade of brown, like mud, but I wore it anyway because she worked so hard on it. The wedding was down at the VFW hall, the one with the leaky roof over the dance floor... \n\nBut yeah, I just know I'm the one who can get everyone on the same page. It's my special gift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that you have a unique role or importance that others don't, like you're meant to lead or guide people in a way no one else can?\n\nPatient: Well, like I keep telling you, Doc, I ain't no president or nothing. But I just got this... this absolute knowing that I'm the one meant to bring the whole neighborhood together. People just naturally listen to me and want to follow the plans I make. \n\nYou gotta have good plans, you know. You can't build a house without a blueprint. My daddy was a carpenter, built half the houses on the south side of town with his own two hands. He used one of those wooden folding rulers, kept it right in his overalls pocket. Those overalls always smelled like pine sawdust and chewing tobacco. Red Man, that was his brand. He'd spit it right into an old Folgers coffee can he kept by the porch steps. Mother hated that can. She used to grow petunias all around the porch, bright pink ones. Said the soil was good there because of the rain runoff from the gutters. We had to clean those gutters out every autumn, wet leaves piled up thick from the big oak tree out front. That tree got struck by lightning back in the summer of '65, split right down the middle like a piece of firewood... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. I just know I'm the one to lead the neighborhood. It's my gift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like others in the neighborhood recognize your ability to lead, or do you think they don’t listen to you as much as they should?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it depends on the week. Some weeks I wake up feeling so bright and important, like I've got the sun right in my chest. I laugh loud, I talk fast, and I'm just ready to get things moving. But if folks don't listen, if they slow me down or start questioning my plans, I get real sharp with them. Real snappy. \n\nIt reminds me of how my old dog Buster used to get. Buster was a beagle mix, had one blue eye and one brown eye. Smartest dog you ever saw, but he had no patience for the neighbor's cat. That cat was a big orange tabby, looked like a pumpkin with legs. It used to sit right on the fence post and just stare at Buster. Used to drive him crazy. Buster would bark until his voice went hoarse. We kept him on a long chain in the yard, attached to this old metal stake my daddy drove into the ground. Daddy used a sledgehammer for that. Heavy thing, probably weighed twenty pounds. He bought it at the hardware store downtown, the one Mr. Henderson used to run before he retired and moved to Florida. Florida's too hot, if you ask me. All that humidity, makes your joints ache. My cousin Martha went to Florida once for a vacation, brought back a whole bag of those little seashell soaps. Looked pretty, but they didn't lather worth a darn... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, when I'm feeling bright and fast, I just get irritable if people don't keep up with me or question what I'm doing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

He had a glass hummingbird feeder on his back porch, used to boil the sugar water himself on the stove... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. He'd bring that heavy old thing out at Christmas and play polkas until my aunt would threaten to hide it in the attic. She kept all her old sewing patterns up in that attic. She used to grow petunias all around the porch, bright pink ones. Said the soil was good there because of the rain runoff from the gutters. We had to clean those gutters out every autumn, wet leaves piled up thick from the big oak tree out front. That tree got struck by lightning back in the summer of '65, split right down the middle like a piece of firewood... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. I just know I'm the one to lead the neighborhood. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I've been trying to tell you, Doc, I don't know about "great things" like being on the television or nothing. But compared to other fellas my age? Sure. I just get this absolute certainty that I'm the only one who can bring this whole neighborhood back together. I've got that special gift, you see, to make people listen and follow my plans. 

Most folks my age, they're just sitting around playing bingo down at the church basement. St. Agnes, over on 4th street. They use those little ink dabbers now, you know? Red and purple ink everywhere. Back in the day, we used those little plastic chips, the translucent red ones. You had to be real careful, because if somebody bumped the folding table, your whole card was ruined. Old man Higgins used to bump the table on purpose if he was losing. He had a terrible temper, Higgins did. Worked at the auto plant until they closed it down in '78. Drove a pale blue Ford Falcon. The muffler on that thing was completely shot, sounded like a tractor coming down the avenue. 

My brother had a tractor, a John Deere. Used it to plow his long gravel driveway in the winter. The winter of '78 was a bad one, let me tell you. Snow was piled all the way up to the porch railing. We had to melt snow on the woodstove just to have water to flush the toilet because the pipes froze solid. Took three days for the county plow to make it out to his place... 

But, anyway, I ain't just sitting around waiting to get old like the rest of them. I got my plans for the neighborhood, and I know I'm the one to do it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22.0, F22.8, F22.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, my wife is the one who wanted me to come talk to you. It’s mostly about the men down at the domino table. I’ve been playing with those boys for... oh, must be twenty years now, ever since I stopped working. Charlie, he always tries to hide the double six, but I catch him. His hands are all curled up with arthritis, poor fella, so he fumbles the tiles a lot. \n\nBut lately... well, I came because they've been slipping in these little messages when they play, and they're meant just for me. And it's not just them, it's the radio announcer too. I listen to the AM station every morning. You know, they don't make radios like they used to. My daddy had one of those big wooden ones, took up half the parlor, had the glowing tubes in the back... \n\nBut anyway, the fella on the radio, he does it too. He gives me messages. My wife, she gets all huffed up and says it is just regular talk, but I know better. I know they are warning me to be careful. I just... I don't know why they can't just come out and say it plain instead of talking in codes.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the messages you receive from the men at the domino table and the radio announcer? Do you believe they are specifically targeting you or trying to harm you in some way?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think they want to hurt me. It's more like... like a warning. Charlie will play a tile, maybe a four-blank, and the way he taps it on the table—tap, tap, tap—it means I need to watch my back. And the radio fella, he'll be reading the farm report or the weather, and he'll pause just a second too long before he says 'storm.' It's meant for me. It's a sign to be careful. \n\nMy wife, bless her heart, she just sighs and tells me to turn the dial. She never did like AM radio anyway. Always preferred her gospel records. We used to have a record player, one of those portable ones in a little suitcase. We took it on a picnic down by the lake back in '62. The water was so clear back then, not like now with all the algae and the motorboats making a racket. We had fried chicken... she made the best fried chicken, cooked it in lard, none of this vegetable oil nonsense they push nowadays. \n\nBut, uh, where was I? Oh, the messages. Yes, even when she says it's just regular talk, I know better. They're telling me to be careful, plain as day. I just wish they wouldn't beat around the bush so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the messages you're receiving are part of a bigger plan or that someone is trying to influence your thoughts or actions in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, a bigger plan... I suppose you could call it that, yes. Like I said, the men at the domino table and the radio announcer, they're giving me these little messages. It's meant for me, to warn me to be careful. My wife tells me it's just talk, but I know it's not. I know it. \n\nSpeaking of plans, though, my wife's been planning to redo the back porch. We've had that same screened-in porch since we bought the place. I put the screens up myself, you know. Bought the mesh down at the old hardware store on Main Street. Old man Henderson ran it back then. He had this big jar of peppermints on the counter, the kind wrapped in clear plastic. You don't see those much anymore. Now everything is in a big box store, no personal touch. Henderson knew exactly what size nails you needed just by looking at the wood you brought in. \n\nBut, uh, yes, the messages. They are definitely trying to tell me something, trying to get me to be careful about... well, whatever it is they're warning me about. I just know it's real, no matter what my wife says.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the messages you're getting are all connected, like they're part of a single plan or scheme that someone has put together specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Well, connected... I suppose they must be. I mean, Charlie at the domino table and the fella on the radio, they're both doing it, right? Giving me those little messages meant just for me. Warning me to be careful. My wife, she just shakes her head and says it's regular talk, but I know what I hear. I know they're warning me. \n\nYou know, speaking of being careful, I used to tell my boys that all the time when they were learning to drive. We had this old Ford pickup, a '54, painted the color of a bruised apple. The clutch on that thing was stiffer than a frozen boot. You had to really stomp on it, or it would grind the gears something awful. My oldest, Tommy, he ground 'em so bad once I thought the whole transmission was gonna fall right out onto the dirt road. We used to drive down to the creek to fish for catfish. Used stink bait... boy, did that stuff smell. Get it on your hands and you wouldn't get it off for a week. \n\nBut yes, doctor, the messages. They're connected, sure as I'm sitting here. They're all trying to tell me the same thing, to watch my step. Even if my wife doesn't hear it, I know they're warning me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the messages you're hearing are being put into your mind by someone or something else, like through the radio or when you're playing dominoes?\n\nPatient: Put into my mind? No, no, they don't have to get inside my head to do it. Like I was telling you, doctor, I came in here because the men at the domino table and sometimes the radio announcer give these little messages meant for me. It's right there out in the open. And even when my wife says it is just talk, I know they are warning me to be careful. I just hear it with my own two ears, plain as day. \n\nMy hearing's always been sharp, you know. Never needed one of those hearing aids. Now my brother Frank, he went deaf as a post by the time he was sixty. Worked in a metal stamping plant up north. Big heavy presses coming down all day long—bam, bam, bam. Shook the whole concrete floor. They didn't give 'em earplugs back then, you just stuffed a bit of cotton in there if your head started ringing too bad. Frank ended up having to read lips mostly. He was a crackerjack mechanic, though. Could rebuild a carburetor blindfolded. Used to lay all the little springs and screws out on a clean rag right on the kitchen table. His wife, Mary, she'd get so mad about the smell of gasoline in the house... she'd chase him out to the garage with a broom. They had a nice garage, detached, with a little wood stove in the corner. Kept it real warm in the winter.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the messages you're hearing are being placed into your thoughts by someone or something else, or do you experience them as coming from outside yourself, like through the radio or during conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, they're not putting thoughts in my head. It's coming from outside, right out in the open. The men at the domino table, the fella on the radio... they just give these little messages meant for me. I hear them talking, warning me to be careful. My wife, she keeps telling me it's just regular talk, but I know what I'm hearing. \n\nSpeaking of hearing things, we used to have this old Zenith radio in the living room. Big wooden cabinet, took up half the wall. You had to wait for the tubes to warm up before you could hear anything. We'd sit around it on Sunday evenings and listen to the programs. Jack Benny, The Lone Ranger... those were the days. My mother would be knitting in her chair, always making these scratchy wool sweaters. I hated wearing them, they made my neck itch terribly. She'd use this dark green yarn she got on sale down at the five and dime. The lady who worked there, Mrs. Gable, she had a glass eye. Always stared right through you. She gave me a piece of hard candy once, but it tasted like mothballs. \n\nBut, uh, no, nobody's putting thoughts in my head. I just hear the messages they're sending me to be careful, plain as day.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the messages you're hearing are part of a special mission or that you have been chosen for a specific purpose?\n\nPatient: Well, a special mission... I don't know if I'd call it a mission, exactly, but sometimes I get so sure that I'm the only one who can bring the whole neighborhood together again. It's like I have this special gift, you know? A gift for making people listen and follow my plans. I just know I can fix things around here if they'd just pay attention. \n\nWe used to have block parties every summer when I was younger. The whole street would come out. Mrs. Higgins would make her famous potato salad. It had too much mustard for my taste, but everyone else seemed to love it. She lived in that blue house on the corner, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree was struck by lightning back in '78. Split it right down the middle, sounded like a cannon going off. Woke the whole street up. The fire department had to come out because a branch fell on old Mr. Peterson's Buick. Ruined the roof completely. He was so mad he tried to sue the city, but you can't sue God for lightning, can you? That's what the judge told him anyway.\n\nBut yeah, I just feel like I've got this... this ability to get people organized, to get them to follow my lead. Like a special gift.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that you have any special powers or abilities that other people don't have?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it's not like I can fly or read minds or anything out of a comic book. But I do get sure, I mean really sure, that I am the one who can bring the whole neighborhood together again. It's like I have a special gift for making people listen and follow my plans. \n\nI was actually trying to explain this to the mailman the other day. He's a new fella, young kid, maybe twenty-two. Drives one of those new electric mail trucks. They're so quiet, you don't even hear them coming down the street. Not like the old ones that used to rattle and sputter. My dog, Buster—God rest his soul—used to hear the mail truck from three blocks away and start howling. Buster was a good dog, part beagle, part something else. We found him shivering under a parked car in the grocery store parking lot. The old A&P that used to be down on Main Street. They tore it down in the nineties to build a pharmacy, which is funny because now there's a pharmacy on every corner. You can't throw a rock without hitting a place selling aspirin and greeting cards. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just have this gift, you know? A way of getting people to see my plans and follow along.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have any kind of special connection with a higher power or that you've been chosen by God or any divine figure for a specific purpose?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I'm not a prophet or anything. I mean, I believe in the good Lord, sure, but He hasn't come down to give me any special orders or tap me on the shoulder. \n\nWe used to go to St. Jude's every Sunday, though. Beautiful old church. They had these big stained glass windows all along the east wall. One of them got broke back in... oh, must have been '89. A bunch of kids playing baseball in the empty lot next door hit a foul ball right through the face of Saint Peter. The priest, Father Thomas, he was furious. He had this little vein in his forehead that would bulge out when he got mad. He drove a Ford Pinto, you know. Pale yellow. Worst car ever made, those Pintos. My brother-in-law Charlie bought one brand new and the heater never worked. Charlie was a pipefitter down at the plant, always had cold hands anyway. He used to carry a big metal thermos of black coffee just to keep his fingers warm on the job. Black coffee, no sugar. Said sugar was for hummingbirds. He had a glass hummingbird feeder on his back porch, used to boil the sugar water himself on the stove... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, God's not giving me any special divine purpose or talking to me from on high. Just my own gift for getting the neighborhood together, that's all.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that you are better or more important than other people, or that you have a role that no one else can fulfill?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about being more important, like I'm a king or a mayor or something. But I do get this feeling, this absolute certainty, that I'm the only one who can bring the whole neighborhood together again. Like I've got this special gift for making folks listen and follow my plans. \n\nIt's sort of like how my Uncle Frank used to get everyone to listen to him. He was a loud man, Frank. Worked on the railroad for forty years, laying track out west. He lost half of his left pinky finger in a coupling accident back in '52. Never stopped him from playing the accordion, though. He'd bring that heavy old thing out at Christmas and play polkas until my aunt would threaten to hide it in the attic. She kept all her old sewing patterns up in that attic. Hundreds of them, mostly from the sixties. She made me a suit once, for my cousin's wedding. It was a terrible shade of brown, like mud, but I wore it anyway because she worked so hard on it. The wedding was down at the VFW hall, the one with the leaky roof over the dance floor... \n\nBut yeah, I just know I'm the one who can get everyone on the same page. It's my special gift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that you have a unique role or importance that others don't, like you're meant to lead or guide people in a way no one else can?\n\nPatient: Well, like I keep telling you, Doc, I ain't no president or nothing. But I just got this... this absolute knowing that I'm the one meant to bring the whole neighborhood together. People just naturally listen to me and want to follow the plans I make. \n\nYou gotta have good plans, you know. You can't build a house without a blueprint. My daddy was a carpenter, built half the houses on the south side of town with his own two hands. He used one of those wooden folding rulers, kept it right in his overalls pocket. Those overalls always smelled like pine sawdust and chewing tobacco. Red Man, that was his brand. He'd spit it right into an old Folgers coffee can he kept by the porch steps. Mother hated that can. She used to grow petunias all around the porch, bright pink ones. Said the soil was good there because of the rain runoff from the gutters. We had to clean those gutters out every autumn, wet leaves piled up thick from the big oak tree out front. That tree got struck by lightning back in the summer of '65, split right down the middle like a piece of firewood... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. I just know I'm the one to lead the neighborhood. It's my gift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like others in the neighborhood recognize your ability to lead, or do you think they don’t listen to you as much as they should?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it depends on the week. Some weeks I wake up feeling so bright and important, like I've got the sun right in my chest. I laugh loud, I talk fast, and I'm just ready to get things moving. But if folks don't listen, if they slow me down or start questioning my plans, I get real sharp with them. Real snappy. \n\nIt reminds me of how my old dog Buster used to get. Buster was a beagle mix, had one blue eye and one brown eye. Smartest dog you ever saw, but he had no patience for the neighbor's cat. That cat was a big orange tabby, looked like a pumpkin with legs. It used to sit right on the fence post and just stare at Buster. Used to drive him crazy. Buster would bark until his voice went hoarse. We kept him on a long chain in the yard, attached to this old metal stake my daddy drove into the ground. Daddy used a sledgehammer for that. Heavy thing, probably weighed twenty pounds. He bought it at the hardware store downtown, the one Mr. Henderson used to run before he retired and moved to Florida. Florida's too hot, if you ask me. All that humidity, makes your joints ache. My cousin Martha went to Florida once for a vacation, brought back a whole bag of those little seashell soaps. Looked pretty, but they didn't lather worth a darn... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, when I'm feeling bright and fast, I just get irritable if people don't keep up with me or question what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel that you are more capable, talented, or destined for great things compared to other people your age?\n\nPatient: Well, like I've been trying to tell you, Doc, I don't know about \"great things\" like being on the television or nothing. But compared to other fellas my age? Sure. I just get this absolute certainty that I'm the only one who can bring this whole neighborhood back together. I've got that special gift, you see, to make people listen and follow my plans. \n\nMost folks my age, they're just sitting around playing bingo down at the church basement. St. Agnes, over on 4th street. They use those little ink dabbers now, you know? Red and purple ink everywhere. Back in the day, we used those little plastic chips, the translucent red ones. You had to be real careful, because if somebody bumped the folding table, your whole card was ruined. Old man Higgins used to bump the table on purpose if he was losing. He had a terrible temper, Higgins did. Worked at the auto plant until they closed it down in '78. Drove a pale blue Ford Falcon. The muffler on that thing was completely shot, sounded like a tractor coming down the avenue. \n\nMy brother had a tractor, a John Deere. Used it to plow his long gravel driveway in the winter. The winter of '78 was a bad one, let me tell you. Snow was piled all the way up to the porch railing. We had to melt snow on the woodstove just to have water to flush the toilet because the pipes froze solid. Took three days for the county plow to make it out to his place... \n\nBut, anyway, I ain't just sitting around waiting to get old like the rest of them. I got my plans for the neighborhood, and I know I'm the one to do it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, Doc, I feel like a broken record here, but yeah, it's just like I said. I don't think I'm better than nobody in a high-and-mighty way, but I do get this absolute certainty that I'm the only one who can bring the whole neighborhood together again. It's like I have a special gift, you know? A gift for making people listen and follow my plans. 

You need a good plan if you're gonna fix things up. Like when they tried to fix up the old bandstand in the park. They hired this contractor from two towns over, fella named Miller or maybe Milner. Drove a shiny new pickup truck, didn't have a speck of dirt on it. Now, you tell me, how's a man gonna do hard work if his truck is that clean? My daddy's truck was always covered in mud and drywall dust. It was a '54 Chevy, step-side. He painted it forest green himself using a brush, not a spray gun. You could see the brush strokes if you looked real close, especially on the tailgate. 

That tailgate was heavy, too. We used to sit on it and eat watermelon in the summer. The kind with the black seeds, not these seedless ones they have now. Seedless watermelon just don't taste right, if you ask me. It's like they bred all the flavor right out of it. We used to have watermelon seed spitting contests. My brother could spit a seed clear across the driveway, hit the side of the garage. The garage had this old tin roof that would rattle like crazy when the wind blew hard. Sounded like a freight train was coming right through the yard. 

But, uh, anyway... yeah. I just know I got that special gift to lead folks around here. I'm the one who can do it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22.0, F22.8, F22.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, my wife is the one who wanted me to come talk to you. It’s mostly about the men down at the domino table. I’ve been playing with those boys for... oh, must be twenty years now, ever since I stopped working. Charlie, he always tries to hide the double six, but I catch him. His hands are all curled up with arthritis, poor fella, so he fumbles the tiles a lot. \n\nBut lately... well, I came because they've been slipping in these little messages when they play, and they're meant just for me. And it's not just them, it's the radio announcer too. I listen to the AM station every morning. You know, they don't make radios like they used to. My daddy had one of those big wooden ones, took up half the parlor, had the glowing tubes in the back... \n\nBut anyway, the fella on the radio, he does it too. He gives me messages. My wife, she gets all huffed up and says it is just regular talk, but I know better. I know they are warning me to be careful. I just... I don't know why they can't just come out and say it plain instead of talking in codes.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the messages you receive from the men at the domino table and the radio announcer? Do you believe they are specifically targeting you or trying to harm you in some way?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I don't think they want to hurt me. It's more like... like a warning. Charlie will play a tile, maybe a four-blank, and the way he taps it on the table—tap, tap, tap—it means I need to watch my back. And the radio fella, he'll be reading the farm report or the weather, and he'll pause just a second too long before he says 'storm.' It's meant for me. It's a sign to be careful. \n\nMy wife, bless her heart, she just sighs and tells me to turn the dial. She never did like AM radio anyway. Always preferred her gospel records. We used to have a record player, one of those portable ones in a little suitcase. We took it on a picnic down by the lake back in '62. The water was so clear back then, not like now with all the algae and the motorboats making a racket. We had fried chicken... she made the best fried chicken, cooked it in lard, none of this vegetable oil nonsense they push nowadays. \n\nBut, uh, where was I? Oh, the messages. Yes, even when she says it's just regular talk, I know better. They're telling me to be careful, plain as day. I just wish they wouldn't beat around the bush so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the messages you're receiving are part of a bigger plan or that someone is trying to influence your thoughts or actions in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, a bigger plan... I suppose you could call it that, yes. Like I said, the men at the domino table and the radio announcer, they're giving me these little messages. It's meant for me, to warn me to be careful. My wife tells me it's just talk, but I know it's not. I know it. \n\nSpeaking of plans, though, my wife's been planning to redo the back porch. We've had that same screened-in porch since we bought the place. I put the screens up myself, you know. Bought the mesh down at the old hardware store on Main Street. Old man Henderson ran it back then. He had this big jar of peppermints on the counter, the kind wrapped in clear plastic. You don't see those much anymore. Now everything is in a big box store, no personal touch. Henderson knew exactly what size nails you needed just by looking at the wood you brought in. \n\nBut, uh, yes, the messages. They are definitely trying to tell me something, trying to get me to be careful about... well, whatever it is they're warning me about. I just know it's real, no matter what my wife says.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the messages you're getting are all connected, like they're part of a single plan or scheme that someone has put together specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Well, connected... I suppose they must be. I mean, Charlie at the domino table and the fella on the radio, they're both doing it, right? Giving me those little messages meant just for me. Warning me to be careful. My wife, she just shakes her head and says it's regular talk, but I know what I hear. I know they're warning me. \n\nYou know, speaking of being careful, I used to tell my boys that all the time when they were learning to drive. We had this old Ford pickup, a '54, painted the color of a bruised apple. The clutch on that thing was stiffer than a frozen boot. You had to really stomp on it, or it would grind the gears something awful. My oldest, Tommy, he ground 'em so bad once I thought the whole transmission was gonna fall right out onto the dirt road. We used to drive down to the creek to fish for catfish. Used stink bait... boy, did that stuff smell. Get it on your hands and you wouldn't get it off for a week. \n\nBut yes, doctor, the messages. They're connected, sure as I'm sitting here. They're all trying to tell me the same thing, to watch my step. Even if my wife doesn't hear it, I know they're warning me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the messages you're hearing are being put into your mind by someone or something else, like through the radio or when you're playing dominoes?\n\nPatient: Put into my mind? No, no, they don't have to get inside my head to do it. Like I was telling you, doctor, I came in here because the men at the domino table and sometimes the radio announcer give these little messages meant for me. It's right there out in the open. And even when my wife says it is just talk, I know they are warning me to be careful. I just hear it with my own two ears, plain as day. \n\nMy hearing's always been sharp, you know. Never needed one of those hearing aids. Now my brother Frank, he went deaf as a post by the time he was sixty. Worked in a metal stamping plant up north. Big heavy presses coming down all day long—bam, bam, bam. Shook the whole concrete floor. They didn't give 'em earplugs back then, you just stuffed a bit of cotton in there if your head started ringing too bad. Frank ended up having to read lips mostly. He was a crackerjack mechanic, though. Could rebuild a carburetor blindfolded. Used to lay all the little springs and screws out on a clean rag right on the kitchen table. His wife, Mary, she'd get so mad about the smell of gasoline in the house... she'd chase him out to the garage with a broom. They had a nice garage, detached, with a little wood stove in the corner. Kept it real warm in the winter.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the messages you're hearing are being placed into your thoughts by someone or something else, or do you experience them as coming from outside yourself, like through the radio or during conversations?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, they're not putting thoughts in my head. It's coming from outside, right out in the open. The men at the domino table, the fella on the radio... they just give these little messages meant for me. I hear them talking, warning me to be careful. My wife, she keeps telling me it's just regular talk, but I know what I'm hearing. \n\nSpeaking of hearing things, we used to have this old Zenith radio in the living room. Big wooden cabinet, took up half the wall. You had to wait for the tubes to warm up before you could hear anything. We'd sit around it on Sunday evenings and listen to the programs. Jack Benny, The Lone Ranger... those were the days. My mother would be knitting in her chair, always making these scratchy wool sweaters. I hated wearing them, they made my neck itch terribly. She'd use this dark green yarn she got on sale down at the five and dime. The lady who worked there, Mrs. Gable, she had a glass eye. Always stared right through you. She gave me a piece of hard candy once, but it tasted like mothballs. \n\nBut, uh, no, nobody's putting thoughts in my head. I just hear the messages they're sending me to be careful, plain as day.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the messages you're hearing are part of a special mission or that you have been chosen for a specific purpose?\n\nPatient: Well, a special mission... I don't know if I'd call it a mission, exactly, but sometimes I get so sure that I'm the only one who can bring the whole neighborhood together again. It's like I have this special gift, you know? A gift for making people listen and follow my plans. I just know I can fix things around here if they'd just pay attention. \n\nWe used to have block parties every summer when I was younger. The whole street would come out. Mrs. Higgins would make her famous potato salad. It had too much mustard for my taste, but everyone else seemed to love it. She lived in that blue house on the corner, the one with the big oak tree in the front yard. That tree was struck by lightning back in '78. Split it right down the middle, sounded like a cannon going off. Woke the whole street up. The fire department had to come out because a branch fell on old Mr. Peterson's Buick. Ruined the roof completely. He was so mad he tried to sue the city, but you can't sue God for lightning, can you? That's what the judge told him anyway.\n\nBut yeah, I just feel like I've got this... this ability to get people organized, to get them to follow my lead. Like a special gift.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that you have any special powers or abilities that other people don't have?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying, it's not like I can fly or read minds or anything out of a comic book. But I do get sure, I mean really sure, that I am the one who can bring the whole neighborhood together again. It's like I have a special gift for making people listen and follow my plans. \n\nI was actually trying to explain this to the mailman the other day. He's a new fella, young kid, maybe twenty-two. Drives one of those new electric mail trucks. They're so quiet, you don't even hear them coming down the street. Not like the old ones that used to rattle and sputter. My dog, Buster—God rest his soul—used to hear the mail truck from three blocks away and start howling. Buster was a good dog, part beagle, part something else. We found him shivering under a parked car in the grocery store parking lot. The old A&P that used to be down on Main Street. They tore it down in the nineties to build a pharmacy, which is funny because now there's a pharmacy on every corner. You can't throw a rock without hitting a place selling aspirin and greeting cards. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I just have this gift, you know? A way of getting people to see my plans and follow along.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have any kind of special connection with a higher power or that you've been chosen by God or any divine figure for a specific purpose?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I'm not a prophet or anything. I mean, I believe in the good Lord, sure, but He hasn't come down to give me any special orders or tap me on the shoulder. \n\nWe used to go to St. Jude's every Sunday, though. Beautiful old church. They had these big stained glass windows all along the east wall. One of them got broke back in... oh, must have been '89. A bunch of kids playing baseball in the empty lot next door hit a foul ball right through the face of Saint Peter. The priest, Father Thomas, he was furious. He had this little vein in his forehead that would bulge out when he got mad. He drove a Ford Pinto, you know. Pale yellow. Worst car ever made, those Pintos. My brother-in-law Charlie bought one brand new and the heater never worked. Charlie was a pipefitter down at the plant, always had cold hands anyway. He used to carry a big metal thermos of black coffee just to keep his fingers warm on the job. Black coffee, no sugar. Said sugar was for hummingbirds. He had a glass hummingbird feeder on his back porch, used to boil the sugar water himself on the stove... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, God's not giving me any special divine purpose or talking to me from on high. Just my own gift for getting the neighborhood together, that's all.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that you are better or more important than other people, or that you have a role that no one else can fulfill?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know about being more important, like I'm a king or a mayor or something. But I do get this feeling, this absolute certainty, that I'm the only one who can bring the whole neighborhood together again. Like I've got this special gift for making folks listen and follow my plans. \n\nIt's sort of like how my Uncle Frank used to get everyone to listen to him. He was a loud man, Frank. Worked on the railroad for forty years, laying track out west. He lost half of his left pinky finger in a coupling accident back in '52. Never stopped him from playing the accordion, though. He'd bring that heavy old thing out at Christmas and play polkas until my aunt would threaten to hide it in the attic. She kept all her old sewing patterns up in that attic. Hundreds of them, mostly from the sixties. She made me a suit once, for my cousin's wedding. It was a terrible shade of brown, like mud, but I wore it anyway because she worked so hard on it. The wedding was down at the VFW hall, the one with the leaky roof over the dance floor... \n\nBut yeah, I just know I'm the one who can get everyone on the same page. It's my special gift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that you have a unique role or importance that others don't, like you're meant to lead or guide people in a way no one else can?\n\nPatient: Well, like I keep telling you, Doc, I ain't no president or nothing. But I just got this... this absolute knowing that I'm the one meant to bring the whole neighborhood together. People just naturally listen to me and want to follow the plans I make. \n\nYou gotta have good plans, you know. You can't build a house without a blueprint. My daddy was a carpenter, built half the houses on the south side of town with his own two hands. He used one of those wooden folding rulers, kept it right in his overalls pocket. Those overalls always smelled like pine sawdust and chewing tobacco. Red Man, that was his brand. He'd spit it right into an old Folgers coffee can he kept by the porch steps. Mother hated that can. She used to grow petunias all around the porch, bright pink ones. Said the soil was good there because of the rain runoff from the gutters. We had to clean those gutters out every autumn, wet leaves piled up thick from the big oak tree out front. That tree got struck by lightning back in the summer of '65, split right down the middle like a piece of firewood... \n\nBut, uh, yeah. I just know I'm the one to lead the neighborhood. It's my gift.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like others in the neighborhood recognize your ability to lead, or do you think they don’t listen to you as much as they should?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it depends on the week. Some weeks I wake up feeling so bright and important, like I've got the sun right in my chest. I laugh loud, I talk fast, and I'm just ready to get things moving. But if folks don't listen, if they slow me down or start questioning my plans, I get real sharp with them. Real snappy. \n\nIt reminds me of how my old dog Buster used to get. Buster was a beagle mix, had one blue eye and one brown eye. Smartest dog you ever saw, but he had no patience for the neighbor's cat. That cat was a big orange tabby, looked like a pumpkin with legs. It used to sit right on the fence post and just stare at Buster. Used to drive him crazy. Buster would bark until his voice went hoarse. We kept him on a long chain in the yard, attached to this old metal stake my daddy drove into the ground. Daddy used a sledgehammer for that. Heavy thing, probably weighed twenty pounds. He bought it at the hardware store downtown, the one Mr. Henderson used to run before he retired and moved to Florida. Florida's too hot, if you ask me. All that humidity, makes your joints ache. My cousin Martha went to Florida once for a vacation, brought back a whole bag of those little seashell soaps. Looked pretty, but they didn't lather worth a darn... \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, when I'm feeling bright and fast, I just get irritable if people don't keep up with me or question what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel that you are more capable, talented, or destined for great things compared to other people your age?\n\nPatient: Well, like I've been trying to tell you, Doc, I don't know about \"great things\" like being on the television or nothing. But compared to other fellas my age? Sure. I just get this absolute certainty that I'm the only one who can bring this whole neighborhood back together. I've got that special gift, you see, to make people listen and follow my plans. \n\nMost folks my age, they're just sitting around playing bingo down at the church basement. St. Agnes, over on 4th street. They use those little ink dabbers now, you know? Red and purple ink everywhere. Back in the day, we used those little plastic chips, the translucent red ones. You had to be real careful, because if somebody bumped the folding table, your whole card was ruined. Old man Higgins used to bump the table on purpose if he was losing. He had a terrible temper, Higgins did. Worked at the auto plant until they closed it down in '78. Drove a pale blue Ford Falcon. The muffler on that thing was completely shot, sounded like a tractor coming down the avenue. \n\nMy brother had a tractor, a John Deere. Used it to plow his long gravel driveway in the winter. The winter of '78 was a bad one, let me tell you. Snow was piled all the way up to the porch railing. We had to melt snow on the woodstove just to have water to flush the toilet because the pipes froze solid. Took three days for the county plow to make it out to his place... \n\nBut, anyway, I ain't just sitting around waiting to get old like the rest of them. I got my plans for the neighborhood, and I know I'm the one to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel that you are better than other people or that you have abilities or importance that others don't?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, I feel like a broken record here, but yeah, it's just like I said. I don't think I'm better than nobody in a high-and-mighty way, but I do get this absolute certainty that I'm the only one who can bring the whole neighborhood together again. It's like I have a special gift, you know? A gift for making people listen and follow my plans. \n\nYou need a good plan if you're gonna fix things up. Like when they tried to fix up the old bandstand in the park. They hired this contractor from two towns over, fella named Miller or maybe Milner. Drove a shiny new pickup truck, didn't have a speck of dirt on it. Now, you tell me, how's a man gonna do hard work if his truck is that clean? My daddy's truck was always covered in mud and drywall dust. It was a '54 Chevy, step-side. He painted it forest green himself using a brush, not a spray gun. You could see the brush strokes if you looked real close, especially on the tailgate. \n\nThat tailgate was heavy, too. We used to sit on it and eat watermelon in the summer. The kind with the black seeds, not these seedless ones they have now. Seedless watermelon just don't taste right, if you ask me. It's like they bred all the flavor right out of it. We used to have watermelon seed spitting contests. My brother could spit a seed clear across the driveway, hit the side of the garage. The garage had this old tin roof that would rattle like crazy when the wind blew hard. Sounded like a freight train was coming right through the yard. \n\nBut, uh, anyway... yeah. I just know I got that special gift to lead folks around here. I'm the one who can do it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

You had to be real careful, because if somebody bumped the folding table, your whole card was ruined. Old man Higgins used to bump the table on purpose if he was losing.
